## 1. Market-share and leadership evidence (tier a / b only)

### 1.1 The J.M. Smucker Company (Smucker's, Double Fruit)

**Form 10-K, fiscal year ended April 30, 2026** — tier (a) — https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/0000091419/000009141926000050/sjm-20260430.htm — opened 26 Sep 2026.

- Canada footprint, verbatim: "Operations outside the U.S. are principally in Canada, although our products are exported to other countries as well."
- "Our corporate headquarters is located in Orrville, Ohio, and our Canadian headquarters is located in Markham, Ontario."
- "We lease four sales and administrative offices in the U.S. and one in Canada." (The properties table lists **no manufacturing location in Canada** for FY2026.)
- Production concentration, verbatim: "We have consolidated our production capacity for certain products into single manufacturing sites, including substantially all of our coffee, Milk-Bone dog snacks, and fruit spreads." Properties table: "Memphis, Tennessee Peanut butter and fruit spreads" (the Orrville, Ohio plant is the principal fruit-spreads site — see 2010 release below).
- Leadership language, verbatim: "We are proud to lead in the coffee, peanut butter, fruit spreads, frozen handheld, sweet baked goods, dog snacks, and cat food categories by offering brands consumers trust for themselves and their families each day, including Folgers, Dunkin', Café Bustelo, Jif, Uncrustables, Smucker's, Hostess, Milk-Bone, and Meow Mix."
- "As a Company of #1 and leading brands, complemented by emerging, on-trend brands, we will continue to drive balanced, long-term growth, primarily in North America."
- Brand/competitor table (U.S. Retail Frozen Handheld and Spreads), verbatim row: "Fruit spreads | Smucker's (A) | Private label brands — Various | Welch's — Welch Foods Inc. | Bonne Maman — Andros Foods USA, Inc." Footnote: "(A) Identifies the current market leader within the product category." **This table is the U.S. retail table; the 10-K makes no Canada-specific fruit-spread share or "#1 in Canada" statement.**
- Private label stance, verbatim: "We closely monitor the price gap, or price premium, between our brands and private label brands, with the view that value is about more than price and the expectation that number one brands will continue to be an integral part of consumers' shopping baskets."
- Geographic net sales (US$ millions): "International: Canada $ 340.8 [FY2026] $ 352.4 [FY2025] $ 429.4 [FY2024]"; "Total net sales $ 9,050.9 $ 8,726.1 $ 8,178.7". Long-lived assets in Canada: $51.1 (FY2026).
- Product-category net sales (US$ millions, all geographies): "Fruit spreads 371.0 [FY2026] 400.8 [FY2025] 427.2 [FY2024] — U.S. Retail Frozen Handheld and Spreads". Volume/mix commentary: "decreases for peanut butter and fruit spreads".
- Canada divestiture, verbatim: "On January 2, 2024, we sold the Canada condiment business to TreeHouse Foods, Inc. ("TreeHouse Foods"). The transaction included Bick's® pickles, Habitant® pickled beets, Woodman's® horseradish, and McLarens® pickled onions brands, inclusive of certain trademarks. Under our ownership, these brands generated net sales of $43.8 in 2024, which was included in the International operating segment."
- Principal trademarks as of April 30, 2026 (segment table) list "Smucker's®" under U.S. Retail Frozen Handheld and Spreads and Away From Home; **"Double Fruit" does not appear in the FY2026 principal-trademark list** (it did appear in the FY2013 list — see below).
- Canadian entity in a filed contract: "Revolving Credit Agreement, dated as of March 7, 2025, among the Company, Smucker Foods of Canada Corp. and certain other subsidiaries of the Company from time to time party thereto, as borrowers…"

**Exhibit 21 (subsidiaries) to the FY2026 10-K** — tier (a) — https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/0000091419/000009141926000050/sjm43026-10kex21.htm — opened 26 Sep 2026. Verbatim: "Smucker Foods of Canada Corp." – Canada. (Only Canadian-named subsidiary listed.)

**Form 10-K, fiscal year ended April 30, 2013** — tier (a) — https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/0000091419/000119312513266625/d537487d10k.htm — opened 26 Sep 2026.
- Canada plant, verbatim: "Canada Locations … Ste. Marie, Quebec (D) Fruit spreads, sweet spreads, and fruit industrial products — International, Foodservice, and Natural Foods"; "(D) The Ste. Marie, Quebec location is expected to close as part of our restructuring plans as described in our 2013 Annual Report to Shareholders on pages 52 and 53 under 'Note 4: Restructuring.'"
- Category position (North American, not Canada-specific), verbatim: "Our fruit spread brands, primarily Smucker's, hold the leading position in the fruit spreads category and compete with Welch's branded line of fruit spreads and many private label brands. The competing brands exist on both a national and a regional level."
- FY2013 trademark list for the International segment included "Bick's®, Five Roses®, Robin Hood®, Carnation®, … Double Fruit®, Recharge®, Red River®, Crosse & Blackwell®, Golden Temple®".

**Smucker news release, 24 March 2010, "The J. M. Smucker Company Announces Plans to Significantly Improve Supply Chain"** — tier (a) — https://s203.q4cdn.com/703080298/files/doc_news/The-J.-M.-Smucker-Company-Announces-Plans-to-Significantly-Improve-Supply-Chain-03-24-2010-2010.pdf — opened 26 Sep 2026 (PDF text extracted).
- "Closing the Memphis, Tennessee, and Ste. Marie, Quebec, fruit spreads plants."
- "Production is anticipated to be phased in through the summer of 2013, at which time the Company expects to close its Memphis and Ste. Marie facilities."
- "Upon completion of the new facility, production of the Company's fruit spreads, syrups, and ice cream toppings for its consumer and foodservice businesses in the U.S. and Canada will primarily be concentrated in Orrville and Ripon."
- Boilerplate names Canadian brands at the time: "Robin Hood(R), Five Roses(R), Carnation(R), Europe's Best(R) and Bick's(R) in Canada."

**smuckers.ca (Smucker Foods of Canada Corp. consumer site)** — tier (a).
- Home https://www.smuckers.ca/en — opened 26 Sep 2026. Product lines listed: "Fruit Spread", "Jelly", "Marmalade Style Spread", "No Sugar Added", "Double Fruit", plus ice-cream toppings. Footer: "© / TM / ® Smucker Foods of Canada Corp. or its affiliates." Tagline: "With a Name Like Smucker's, It Has to Be Good®". **No "Canada's favourite", "#1" or similar Canada ranking claim appears anywhere on the site pages opened.**
- Sitemap https://www.smuckers.ca/sitemap.xml (opened) shows the Canadian range uses the common names "Strawberry Spread", "Raspberry Spread", "Apple Jelly", "Grape Jelly", "Orange Marmalade Style Spread", "No Sugar Added Strawberry", "Double Fruit Strawberry Spread". **No product on smuckers.ca carries the common name "Jam".**
- About https://www.smuckers.ca/en/about-us — opened. Verbatim: "It all started in 1897, when Jerome Monroe Smucker pressed cider from his mill and, shortly after, sold his first jar of apple butter from the back of a horse-drawn wagon." "Today, Smucker's products include a broad range of fruit spreads, ice cream toppings and peanut butter including natural, low sugar and sugar free varieties." "Now on our fifth generation of Smucker family leadership". No Canada-specific heritage statement.
- FAQ https://www.smuckers.ca/en/faq — opened. Legal-name definition, verbatim: "FRUIT SPREADS … there are no requirements for (brix) sweetness or fruit content in fruit spreads. A fruit spread is a product that is not a jam, jelly or marmalade." Ingredient questions are routed to "Consumer Relations Department at 1-800-567-1897". **No FAQ entry on glucose-fructose or on where products are made.**
- Product pages opened: https://www.smuckers.ca/en/jellies-fruit-spreads/fruit-spread/strawberry-spread ("Families have loved our Strawberry Spread for more than 100 years…"); https://www.smuckers.ca/en/jellies-fruit-spreads/double-fruit/strawberry-spread ("Strawberry Fruit Spread"; "Packed with twice the fruit" plus a sugar/calorie comparison clause — not reproduced, out of scope; "Made with pieces of real strawberries."). **Ingredient lists and origin statements are not rendered as text on these pages** (label images only) — ingredient order must be taken from the physical label.

The street address "80 Whitehall Drive, Markham, ON L3R 0P3" appears only in tier (c) directories (Panjiva, Yelp, YellowPages, opengovca "corporation #7557744") — flagged, see unverified section; confirm on https://www.smuckers.ca/en/contact-us (not opened) or Corporations Canada.

### 1.2 E.D. Smith (brand now owned by Winland Foods / Investindustrial)

**Globe and Mail, 25 June 2007, John Partridge, "E.D. Smith to be sold to Treehouse Foods for $217-million"** — tier (b) — https://www.theglobeandmail.com/report-on-business/ed-smith-to-be-sold-to-treehouse-foods-for-217-million/article20404793/ — opened 26 Sep 2026.
- Buyer: "TreeHouse Foods Inc. of Westchester, Ill., will buy substantially all of its assets" for $217 million; seller E.D. Smith Income Fund.
- History: incorporated 1882, roots to 1878, founder Ernest D'Israeli Smith "then a bachelor farmer, later a married M.P. and then Senator, began growing strawberries at his property in Ontario's Niagara Peninsula"; HQ Winona, Ontario; ~800 employees; described as an "iconic Canadian food processor". The article gives **no jam market-share or "Canada's largest jam" statement**.

**CBC News, 25 June 2007, "Jam maker E.D. Smith relishes takeover by U.S. pickle giant"** — tier (b) — https://www.cbc.ca/news/business/jam-maker-e-d-smith-relishes-takeover-by-u-s-pickle-giant-1.669471 — opened 26 Sep 2026.
- "E.D. Smith Income Fund on Monday said it has accepted an all-cash $217-million friendly takeover offer from Chicago's TreeHouse Foods Inc. — a maker of pickles and relishes."
- "A maker of jams, ketchup and sauces, E.D. Smith has production facilities in Winona, Seaforth, and Cambridge, Ont., and in Pennsylvania. It was founded 125 years ago in Winona — a town in the Niagara Peninsula. The firm employs about 800 people."
- "E.D. Smith's headquarters will remain in Winona."
- Interim CEO Martin Thrasher: "Canada represents a whole new market for TreeHouse and, in the U.S., our combined resources truly muscles up the business for both of us."

**Food Processing (trade outlet), 27 June 2007, "TreeHouse Foods to acquire Canadian jam manufacturer"** — tier (b) — https://www.foodprocessing.com/on-the-plant-floor/plant-operations-and-engineering/news/11342429/food-industry-news-treehouse-foods-to-acquire-canadian-jam-manufacturer-food-processing — opened 26 Sep 2026.
- "In Canada, it is the leading private label manufacturer in nine key dry grocery categories"; "In the U.S., it is the leading producer of private label salad dressings"; revenue "approximately $245 million" for the 12 months to March 31, 2007; plants Winona, Seaforth, Cambridge (Ont.) and North East, Pennsylvania; ~800 employees.

**TreeHouse Foods news release, 3 October 2022, "TreeHouse Foods completes sale of significant portion of its Meal Preparation business to Investindustrial"** — tier (a) — https://www.treehousefoods.com/news-and-media/press-release-details/2022/TREEHOUSE-FOODS-COMPLETES-SALE-OF-SIGNIFICANT-PORTION-OF-ITS-MEAL-PREPARATION-BUSINESS-TO-INVESTINDUSTRIAL/default.aspx — opened 26 Sep 2026.
- "$950 million" for "a significant portion of its Meal Preparation business"; "The divested business has been renamed Winland Foods, Inc." **The release does not name E.D. Smith or Winona.**
- The 10 Aug 2022 announcement (same site, NOT OPENED; SEC 8-K copy at https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/1320695/000132069522000167/pressrelease.htm NOT OPENED) is reported in search snippets to describe the divested business as making "pasta, red sauces, salsa, salad dressings, mayonnaise, condiments, syrup, pie-fillings and preserves" with "14 manufacturing facilities located in the US, Canada and Italy" — snippet only, see unverified.

**Investindustrial portfolio page "Windoria"** — tier (a) (owner's own page) — https://www.investindustrial.com/our-business/portfolio-overview/current-portfolio/Windoria.html — opened 26 Sep 2026.
- "Windoria is a leading global private label food manufacturing platform, formed by the combination of La Doria in Europe (acquired in May 2022) and the majority of the Treehouse foods meal preparation division in the US (acquired October 2022) and renamed Winland Foods."
- "operates 28 manufacturing plants in the US, Canada, and Italy and an innovative logistics platform in the UK." Product categories named: "mainly pasta, sauces & tomato derivatives, condiments and canned legumes and vegetables." **E.D. Smith is not named on this page.**

**Ownership chain (documented):** E.D. Smith Income Fund → TreeHouse Foods Inc. (2007, C$217M, Globe/CBC) → Winland Foods, Inc. (Investindustrial, October 2022, US$950M, TreeHouse release). The Canadian operating entity name "Winland Foods Canada, Inc." appears only in tier (c) sources (Justia trademarks, D&B) — flagged. **E.D. Smith's own site could not be opened (503), so its heritage wording ("1882", "1904 first pure jam"), any "#1"/"most trusted" claim, and the legal name printed on current labels are unverified here.**

"He convinced Gabi that moving to Parry Sound, a small Northern Ontario community, was simply the right thing to do." "1989 The couple take the plunge and buy a former Laundromat for their fruit-filled dream." "1991 The fruit spread is born! They push spread-boundaries by sticking to their organic guns." Gerhard "visits our partner farms often. Even in China!"

**Frontenac release, 22 Jan 2026, "Frontenac Completes Exit of Crofter's Organic"** — tier (a) — https://frontenac.com/frontenac-completes-exit-of-crofters-organic/ — opened 26 Sep 2026. - On pectin (from the same page, as summarised by the fetch tool — verify wording on page): most recipes rely on naturally occurring fruit pectin, while some products have added pectin for consistency; fruit is sourced "as locally as possible" and otherwise from "trusted growers". **The company page therefore does not make a flat "no pectin" claim; "no pectin or preservatives are added" appears only on tier (c) reseller/blog pages (flagged).**
- Ownership: not the Greaves family since 1989; "two local families". Names of the 1989 purchasers appear only in tier (c) sources (flagged).

**Ownership:** family company (Limoges family) per its own pages and the 2023 announcement; legal name "Pied-Mont Dora Inc." per company site and Aliments du Québec registry. **The legal name as printed on retail labels was not verified** (Walmart.ca and Mayrand product pages could not be read).

### 1.10 Last Mountain Berry Farms (Southey, Saskatchewan)

**lmbf.com (company site)** — tier (a).
- Our story https://lmbf.com/ourstory/ — opened 26 Sep 2026. Founded 1989 as "Wildlife Farms" by Barbara and Barry Isaac after Barbara tasted saskatoon berries in 1988 ("someone should do something with this fruit"); on a family farm homesteaded by Edgar Hillier in 1905; first trade show 1991; by 2003 "6 flavours of jam in 3 different sizes" (saskatoon, blueberry, blackberry, strawberry, raspberry, red cherry) plus saskatoon pie filling; by 2004 in Costco, Walmart, Loblaws and Sobeys "across nearly the whole country".
- Home https://lmbf.com/ — opened. Claims: "Lower sugar content"; "More true fruit flavour"; "Simple ingredients"; "straightforward recipes with fewer additives". Shop https://lmbf.com/shop/ — opened: jam-type spreads (Apricot, Blackberry, Blueberry, Blueberry Saskatoon, Haskap Berry, Raspberry, Red Cherry, Rhubarb Strawberry, Saskatoon, Strawberry, Strawberry/Sour Cherry/Pomegranate) at $7.99 each; Saskatoon Pie Filling $8.49; Raspberry and Saskatoon Berry Syrup $7.99. Product page https://lmbf.com/product/saskatoon/ — opened: described as a "jam-type spread"; $7.99; **no ingredient list rendered**.

**Fruit & Vegetable Magazine (Annex Business Media trade title), 31 Mar 2008, "Success runs wild at Saskatchewan's Last Mountain Berry Farms"** — tier (b) — https://www.fruitandveggie.com/success-runs-wild-at-saskatchewans-last-mountain-berry-farms-1365/ — opened 26 Sep 2026. "the nation's largest processor of saskatoon berries"; "4,000 quarts of jam and spread" per day; retailers "Sobeys, Loblaws and Costco"; "no chemicals or added preservatives"; about 250,000 litres of fruit a year from Saskatchewan, Manitoba, Alberta and British Columbia; "between 20 and 25 pallets" weekly.

**Costco listing today:** not verified on costco.ca (search surfaced only a tier (c) review naming item 463139, 950 mL Old Fashioned Raspberry Jam, $8.49 — flagged).

Supplying Loblaw, Metro, Sobeys, Walmart, Farm Boy and 3,000+ independents coast to coast."; "S&F Food Importers supplies Loblaw, Metro, Sobeys, Walmart, Farm Boy and independent grocers across Canada with specialty confectionery, canned goods, baking essentials and tea — sourced from trusted European producers since 1976." **Podravka is not named in the page text captured**; /about-us returned 404. The Podravka–S&F link is unverified (see final section).

"By taking the lead in sourcing and formulating new products, co-branding with premium national and international brands, and partnering with top manufacturers around the world, we've created an exclusive range of products…" "Every product that carries the Kirkland Signature name is carefully researched, tested, hand-selected, or custom-created by a dedicated team of experts here at Costco."

## 3. Google Trends — Canada, past 12 months — tier (c) SUPPORTING SIGNAL ONLY

Pulled 26 Sep 2026, 19:57–20:02 UTC via trends.google.com API (geo = CA, time = "today 12-m", web search, all categories). Weekly series covers "Sep 21 – 27, 2025" to "Sep 20 – 26, 2026" (53 points). Values are Google's 0–100 relative index within each comparison set; "avg" is the mean of the 53 weekly values, "peak" the maximum. **Caveats:** search interest is not sales; Google treats "Smucker's" and "Smuckers" as different strings (see set 6); "ED Smith" and "Welch's" are ambiguous (people named Ed Smith; Welch's juice/snacks); private-label jams are rarely searched by name. Raw JSON saved in the scratchpad as gt_set1…gt_set9.json.

| Set | Terms (Canada) | avg (peak) |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Smucker's / Bonne Maman / E.D. Smith / Crofter's / St. Dalfour | 0.4 / **41.5** / 0.5 / 0.0 / 0.0 |
| 6 | Smucker's / **Smuckers** / ED Smith jam / Kirkland jam / no name jam | 0.9 (36) / **57.9 (100)** / 0.1 (3) / 0.0 (2) / 0.1 (3) |
| 7 | Smuckers / Bonne Maman / ED Smith / Crofters / Kraft jam | 20.5 (35) / **32.2 (78)** / 26.1 (100)* / 0.9 (12) / 0.5 (13) |
| 8 | Smuckers / Bonne Maman / Welch's jelly / Kirkland jam / St Dalfour | 26.4 (46) / **41.5 (100)** / 0.0 (1) / 0.0 (1) / 1.3 (17) |
| 9 | Smuckers jam / Bonne Maman jam / ED Smith jam / Crofters jam / Greaves jam | 13.9 (85) / **18.4 (100)** / 0.1 (6) / 0.7 (20) / 2.0 (85) |
| 4 | Smucker's / E.D. Smith / Crofter's / Kraft jam / Greaves jam | 2.1 (85) / 2.5 (90) / 0.1 (6) / 3.6 (89) / 2.4 (100) |
| 5 | Smucker's / St. Dalfour / Dora jam / Last Mountain jam / Welch's | 0.6 (25) / 0.0 (2) / 0.1 (4) / 0.0 (0) / 53.2 (100)** |
| 2 | Bonne Maman / Greaves jam / Last Mountain Berry Farms / Dora confiture / Welch's jelly | 41.5 / 0.5 / 0.0 / 0.0 / 0.0 |
| 3 | Bonne Maman / Kraft jam / Kirkland jam / Compliments jam / Great Value jam | 41.5 / 0.7 / 0.0 / 0.0 / 0.0 |

\* "ED Smith" without the jam qualifier is confounded by unrelated people; with "jam" it drops to ~0.1. \*\* "Welch's" is dominated by juice/snack searches; "Welch's jelly" ≈ 0.

**Tier (c) reading:** Bonne Maman > Smuckers (≈ 0.6–0.8× Bonne Maman depending on spelling) >> Greaves jam ≈ Kraft jam ≈ ED Smith jam (all low single digits) > Crofters > St Dalfour > Dora, Last Mountain, Kirkland, Compliments, Great Value, no name (≈ 0).

## 4. Corporate claims — verbatim, with URL (all opened 26 Sep 2026 unless noted)

**Smucker's (smuckers.ca)** — "It all started in 1897…" (about-us); range: "Fruit Spread", "Jelly", "Marmalade Style Spread", "No Sugar Added", "Double Fruit" (home/sitemap); Double Fruit: "Packed with twice the fruit…" and "Made with pieces of real strawberries." (double-fruit/strawberry-spread); FAQ legal-name statement: "A fruit spread is a product that is not a jam, jelly or marmalade." **No glucose-fructose FAQ; no origin statement rendered; no Canada ranking claim.** Footer legal name: "Smucker Foods of Canada Corp." Production: fruit spreads for "the U.S. and Canada" concentrated in Orrville, Ohio and Ripon, Wisconsin (2010 release); no Canadian plant in FY2026 10-K.

**E.D. Smith** — company pages unreachable (503). Documented externally: founded 1882 with roots to 1878, Winona, Ontario (Globe, CBC 2007); owned by Winland Foods (Investindustrial) since October 2022 (TreeHouse release). Claims "1904 first Canadian pure jam", "#1", "most trusted", "prepared in Winona", "employs 240" — **unverified** (only Wikipedia/madeinca.ca/search snippets).

**Bonne Maman** — "Simple Ingredients, Homemade Taste"; "The recipe? Just fruit, sugar, and lemon juice — nothing more" (bonnemaman.ca our-story); "manufactured by Andros (a general partnership), located in the Industrial Zone, 46130 Biars-sur-Cère, France" and distributed in Canada by "Meilleures Marques Ltd." (bonnemaman.ca terms); "Bonne Maman, créée en 1971" and Andros "créée en 1959", "Entreprise familiale et multi-locale" (andros.fr history); INTENSE line "more fruit and 38% less sugar than the common jam" (Canadian Grocer sponsored/listing, 2020).

**St. Dalfour** — "The method dates to the First World War, when sugar was scarce in France and preserve-makers sweetened fruit with concentrated grape must instead."; "100% fruit, sweetened with concentrated grape must"; "crafted in Marmande, near Bordeaux in southwest France"; "French heritage dating back to 1903"; "60 Millions de pots, 100 % fabriqués à Marmande"; "présents dans plus de 80 pays" (stdalfour.com / stdalfour.fr). Canadian importer Husky Food Importers (Vaughan, ON; founded 1967) — its copy says "Loire Valley", contradicting the maker's Marmande statement.

**Crofter's** — "one-third less sugar than a preserve"; "purely organic and non-gmo"; "Family owned SINCE 1989"; Parry Sound story; pectin "made from apples or citrus"; "organic cane sugar"; farmers/suppliers "in nearly a dozen different countries"; "Private Label / Co Pack" service (croftersorganic.com). "Fair trade" is not a company claim on the pages opened — it appears only in customer testimonials. "Largest supplier of organic jams, jellies, and spreads in North America" and "fastest-growing brand in the category" are the **acquirer's** (Power Sustainable) statements, 22 Jan 2026.

**Greaves** — "Established 1927"; "hand-stirring each batch of jams in open stainless-steel kettles"; "over 40 mouthwatering varieties of pure, all-natural preserves"; "no artificial ingredients"; "using only fruit and sugar in many of those same time-honoured recipes"; "10,000 sqft factory in Niagara-on-the-Lake … distribution across Canada"; owned since 1989 by "two local families" (greavesjams.com/company/). Pectin: natural fruit pectin in most recipes, added pectin in some — **not a blanket "no pectin" claim on the company page.**

**Dora (Pied-Mont Dora Inc.)** — "TRULY FROM HERE, SINCE 1945"; "Founded in 1945 by the Limoges family in Sainte-Anne-des-Plaines, Quebec as 'Conserves des Laurentides'"; jams since 1960; found "throughout Quebec, as well as in other provinces across Canada and even in the United States"; Authentik "Contain 55% of fruits", "No conservatives, artificial colors or flavors"; More Fruits "50% more fruits"; "Aliments préparés au Québec" certification (Aliments du Québec registry); "usine de 65,000 pieds carrés" (2023 announcement).

**Last Mountain Berry Farms** — founded 1989 as Wildlife Farms by Barbara and Barry Isaac, Southey, SK; in Costco, Walmart, Loblaws, Sobeys by 2004 (lmbf.com/ourstory); "Lower sugar content"; "Simple ingredients"; "fewer additives"; products sold as "jam-type spread" (lmbf.com). Trade press 2008: "the nation's largest processor of saskatoon berries".

**Kraft** — "Kraft PURE Strawberry Jam with Pectin"; "Proudly Prepared in Canada"; "contain No Preservatives"; "contains real fruit ingredients" (Kraft Heinz Away From Home Canada); legal entity on kraftheinz.com/en-CA: "Kraft Heinz Canada, ULC".

**Welch's** — "over 600 American farming families, cultivating the mighty Concord grape" (welchs.com).

**Kirkland Signature** — "Made in Canada from Imported Ingredients" (Costco Business Centre); brand "partnering with top manufacturers around the world" (costco.ca).

## 5. Proposed popularity ranking of mass-market jam brands in Canada (most → least popular), with evidence and confidence

No source gives Canadian brand shares; nothing below is a share estimate. Confidence labels: **documented** = tier (a)/(b) statement directly about Canadian standing; **proxy** = inferred from documented scale, distribution, or the tier (c) Trends signal; **guess** = ordering judgment only.

1. **President's Choice / no name (Loblaw)** — proxy. Loblaw is "Canada's food and pharmacy leader" (own release, 2022); private label held an 18.6% dollar share of Canadian grocery in 2017 (Canadian Grocer/Nielsen 2018); no jam-SKU data. Trends ≈ 0 (not searched by name).
2. **Smucker's** — proxy (strong). Category leader in Smucker's own U.S. table and "leading position in the fruit spreads category" (10-K FY2013); Canada net sales US$340.8M across all products (FY2026); long Canadian presence (Culinar/Double Fruit 1993; Ste-Marie plant to c. 2014); second-highest Trends interest in Canada ("Smuckers"). Canada-specific share: not documented.
3. **Bonne Maman** — proxy (strong). Highest Google Trends interest of any jam brand in Canada over the past 12 months (tier c); national distribution via Meilleures Marques/Recipe Unlimited; INTENSE launch "nationwide" (2020). No Canadian share or growth number documented.
4. **E.D. Smith** — proxy. Canada's oldest national jam brand (1882, Winona; Globe/CBC); "leading private label manufacturer in nine key dry grocery categories" in Canada in 2007 (Food Processing); Winland snippet "leading National Brand in Canada" is unverified. Trends for "ED Smith jam" ≈ 0.1 — weak consumer search interest.
5. **Compliments (Sobeys/Empire)** — proxy (parent scale; ~3,400 Compliments items). No jam data.
6. **Great Value (Walmart Canada)** — proxy (parent scale; 1,100 GV products in 2009). No jam data.
7. **Selection (Metro)** — proxy (Metro: Quebec/Ontario only; "best value for the price" tier). No jam data.
8. **Kirkland Signature Organic Strawberry Spread (Costco)** — proxy. Single SKU, club channel only; "Unavailable" at the selected warehouse on costco.ca on 26 Sep 2026.
9. **Kraft Pure Jam (Kraft Heinz Canada)** — guess/proxy. Still "Proudly Prepared in Canada" and listed in foodservice cups; retail 500 mL listings exist but could not be opened; jam absent from the Kraft Heinz 10-K; Trends "Kraft jam" ≈ 0.5–3.6.
10. **Crofter's Organic** — proxy. "Largest supplier of organic jams … in North America" and "fastest-growing brand in the category" per its acquirer (Jan 2026); organic niche; Trends "Crofters" ≈ 0.9.
11. **St. Dalfour** — guess. Imported via Husky Foods; listed at Loblaws, Walmart, Safeway (listings not opened); Trends ≈ 1.3.
12. **Welch's (jelly/jam)** — guess. U.S. #2 competitor per Smucker 10-K; Canadian listings at Walmart/Metro (not opened); "Welch's jelly" Trends ≈ 0; importer undocumented.
13. **Greaves** — guess (regional, Niagara; "distribution across Canada" per company; 10,000 sq ft plant). Trends "Greaves jam" ≈ 2.0 — comparable to Kraft jam.
14. **Dora (Pied-Mont Dora)** — guess (regional). Deep Quebec listings (Metro, Maxi, Provigo, IGA, Walmart, Costco…) plus Fortinos/No Frills Ottawa; 65,000 sq ft plant; Trends ≈ 0.1 nationally.
15. **Last Mountain Berry Farms** — guess (regional, Prairies). Costco/Sobeys/Loblaws per own 2004 claim and 2008 trade press; Trends ≈ 0.

Read least→most popular for the script by reversing the list. Ranks 5–8 (retailer brands) are ordered purely by parent-retailer scale and could be reordered without contradicting any source.

Market research and media not usable
- GlobalData ("CAD 252.50 Million in 2023, CAGR 3.63% 2018–2023"), ResearchAndMarkets, Statista ("sales value of jam, jelly, marmalade Canada 2016"), IndexBox, Mordor Intelligence, Cognitive Market Research — tier (c) teasers; DO NOT USE.
- Chatelaine "Best Grocery-Store Strawberry Jam in Canada" — blocked (403); ranking not captured.
- Hamilton Spectator "THE SWEET SPOT" (27 Aug 2026) — blocked (403); relevance unknown.
- Costcuisine reviews (Kirkland spread; Last Mountain at Costco), madeinca.ca, canadianbrandsdb.com, Amazon/Cooklist copy ("100% fresh, never frozen, organic strawberries", "40% more strawberries") — tier (c).
- Google Trends figures in section 3 — tier (c) supporting signal only; not evidence of sales.

# JAM — STRUCTURE NOTE (from the six videos that worked, plus the comments on the two jam videos)

Sources read in full (music/noise tags stripped before counting): Canadian Counter "7 Jam Brands Sold in Canada You MUST AVOID (And 2 That Are Actually Worth It)" (80K, 16 Apr 2026, 3,271 words / 20,286 chars); Canada Eats "6 Canadian Jam Brands to Never Buy And 2 That Are Great" (27.6K, 35x median, 3 May 2026, 2,417 w / 14,960 c); Canada Eats peanut butter (149K, 188x, 2,062 w / 12,448 c); Consumer Exposed peanut butter (113K, US, 4,134 w / 25,396 c); Consumer Exposed jam (21K, US, 4,312 w / 25,896 c); Canadian Counter peanut butter (24.5K, 3,286 w / 20,796 c). Comments: jam_comments.md (125 + 54 comments). Abbreviations below: CC = Canadian Counter, CE = Canada Eats, CX = Consumer Exposed.

Headline number for the budget: the 80K CC jam script ran 20,286 characters with tags removed. The 21,000–23,000 target is that video plus one beat (the yardstick/interlude) and a proper close.

---

## 1. THE SKELETON COMMON TO THE VIDEOS THAT WORKED

### 1a. Cold open (50–180 words). Four moves in order: the shelf lie, "it's in your kitchen", the count, the skip-ahead hook.
- CC jam (178 w): "The jam aisle in the Canadian grocery store is one of the most misleading stretches of shelf space in the entire building." → "Seven brands on the avoid list today, two that earned genuine worth it placements." → three teasers: "One of the avoid brands was founded by a man who sold his first products from a horse-drawn wagon, and the company that now sells under his name puts three separate sweetener systems into a single jar of strawberry jam." / "E.D. Smith, another avoid brand, built its entire identity around the appearance of homemade artisan production and sells in over 125 countries." / "And the healthiest recommendation on this list uses a sweetening method that traces to the First World War" → "Let's start with the avoid list."
- CE jam (74 w): "The average jar of jam on a Canadian grocery shelf lists fruit second or third behind sugar and glucose-fructose. Canada's own food law requires 45% fruit in anything legally called jam, but some of the most trusted brands in the country are barely clearing that bar. And one of them literally put the word pure in the product name while listing glucose-fructose as the second ingredient. Here is what is actually in the jar."
- CE peanut butter (49 w): "Most Canadians buy peanut butter on autopilot. But flip that label over and some of the country's most trusted brands are hiding hydrogenated oil, corn syrup fillers, and multiple sweeteners alongside the peanuts. Here are the six worst offenders on Canadian shelves and two that are actually worth buying."
- CX peanut butter (129 w): "Today, we are exposing the peanut butter brands that have been misleading families for decades. And most people have no idea." → "We start with the ones to avoid, then reveal the three that are actually clean. The worst one on this list is probably already in your kitchen. Let's get into it."
- CX jam (109 w): "The jar of jam in your kitchen right now, there's a real chance it has never actually been made from fruit." → "Flip it over and read the first three ingredients." → "Same stores, similar prices, completely different products. Let's get into it."
- CC peanut butter (153 w): "Peanut butter is one of the most purchased pantry staples in Canada, and it is also one of the most misunderstood products on the grocery shelf." → "Eight brands on the avoid list, two that earned genuine worth it placements." → three teasers ("One brand on this list was pulled from Canadian shelves entirely in 2017…", "Another caused a salmonella recall…", "And the healthiest recommendation is a small family operation in Ontario…").
- → The two devices that recur in every winner: a count with the worth-it number attached ("seven… and two"), and a specific tease of the last brand without naming it. CE's law number ("45% fruit") is the only open that gives the viewer a yardstick they can carry through the whole video; it is the strongest open in the set and it is Canadian.

### 1b. Count and order
- Every winner counts DOWN and puts the most trusted brand last. CE jam: "At number six, we have Great Value" … "At number one, we have Smucker's". CE peanut butter: "At number one, the worst peanut butter brand in Canada is Kraft." CX peanut butter: "Number seven, Great Value." … "Number one. Jif." CX jam: "Number six, Welch's Concord grape Jelly." … "Number one, Smuckers Strawberry Jam."
- The ladder is explicitly price/trust ascending. CE jam, end of #6: "Great Value sits at number six not because it is the best option, but because it is the most honest about what it is. It is a budget jam. The danger is assuming that paying more automatically buys you something better because as we move up this list, that assumption falls apart fast." CX jam, before #3: "That's four brands down. We're about to get to the two where the problems cut a little deeper."
- CC does NOT number. It uses ordinal prose: "The brand that defined the jams and jellies category for most North Americans for over a century opens this investigation." / "The second brand on this avoid list…" / "The third brand requires almost no corporate history…" / "The final brand on the avoid list is the one that will generate the most disagreement…". CC jam puts the most famous brand (Smucker's) FIRST; CC peanut butter also leads with the best-seller ("The brand that Canadians reach for most… earns it the top spot on this list"). Both CC videos therefore spend their biggest name in the first three minutes.
- Both CC videos then lose the count: CC jam says "Before the final brand on the avoid list, a note…" and then delivers two more brands (Kraft, Greaves). CC peanut butter says "Subscribe before the final two brands" correctly. The new script numbers every beat so this cannot happen.
- Counts used: 6+2 (CE ×2), 7+3 (CX pb), 6+5 (CX jam), 7+2 (CC jam), 8+2 (CC pb). Seven avoid + two worth-it is the proven CC shape and matches the title.

### 1c. Per-brand beat anatomy (measured)
Beat lengths, avoid list: CE jam 237–342 w (median ~270); CE pb 201–339 w; CX pb 197–568 w (escalating: #3 423, #2 482, #1 568); CX jam 293–366 w; CC jam 246–368 w (median ~306); CC pb 224–357 w. Worth-it beats: CE jam 183–188 w; CE pb 209–245 w; CC jam 353 and 403 w; CX pb 292–403 w. → Target for this script: avoid beats 250–320 w, #1 allowed to run to ~350; worth-it beats 250–280 w each.

The nine elements, with the line that does each job:
a. Recognition hook / where it sits on the shelf. CE jam: "At number six, we have Great Value, Walmart Canada's house jam. And to be upfront, Great Value has never pretended to be anything more than a budget option." CX pb: "This is the one you grab when the budget is tight. No mascot, no heritage story, no tagline." CX jam: "This is the one that's been in your family for generations." CE jam #2: "At number two, we have E.D. Smith, and this one hits differently for Canadian shoppers." CC jam #7: "The final brand on the avoid list is the one that will generate the most disagreement from Canadian shoppers who have visited the store where it is made and bought it as a souvenir of an afternoon in wine country. The disagreement is understandable. The avoid placement stands."
b. Owner and banners. CE jam: "At number four, we have Compliments, Sobeys house brand, sold at Sobeys, Safeway, IGA, and FreshCo locations across Canada." CE pb: "No Name is Loblaws stripped-down value label, the yellow and black packaging you find at Sears, Real Canadian Superstore, and independent grocery stores from coast to coast."
c. Price, jar size stated. CE jam: "At roughly $3 for a 500 ml jar, the price is the pitch." CE pb: "Typically priced around $5 for a 500 g jar, which is not even meaningfully cheaper than some of the better brands on this list." CE pb (the model per-100 g line): "A 1.13 kg jar typically runs around 10 to 12 dollars at Costco Canada, which works out to roughly $1 per 100 g. Compare that to Kraft at closer to $1.10 per 100 g". CC jam gives no prices anywhere; a commenter supplied one ("No Name jam is $7.99 in Canada! Use to be $4.00").
d. "Flip the jar" + the declaration read verbatim, with its source named. CE jam: "The official Compliments Pure Strawberry Jam ingredient declaration from compliments.ca reads, "Sugars, glucose, sugar, strawberries, citric acid, fruit pectin."" / "The confirmed ingredient list for Smucker's Pure Strawberry Jam sold in Canada, "Strawberries, glucose-fructose, sugar, citric acid, pectin."" CE pb: "Turn the jar over and the ingredient list reads dry roasted peanuts, icing sugar, hydrogenated vegetable oils, that is rapeseed, cottonseed, and soybean, salt, and molasses." CX jam: "Flip the jar over. Strawberries are listed first, but immediately behind them, high fructose corn syrup, then corn syrup again, then plain sugar." CC jam: "Smucker's strawberry jam contains strawberries, high fructose corn syrup, corn syrup, sugar, fruit pectin, and citric acid. Count the sweeteners."
e. The ingredient-order rule stated once, as law not opinion. CX jam: "Ingredient lists are written in descending order by weight. Whatever appears first, there's the most of it in the jar." CE jam: "Canadian ingredient panels are ordered by weight, highest to lowest."
f. The concession (every winner does it, every brand). CC jam: "which is a genuinely Canadian manufacturing detail worth acknowledging" (E.D. Smith); "No Name does not pretend to be something it is not, which is more than can be said for some brands on this list."; "The formulation objectively is cleaner than Smucker's." (Kraft); "Greaves is the most authentically crafted product on this entire avoid list." CE jam: "The current label appears to have been cleaned up since then, which is the only reason this brand lands at four rather than higher." CE pb: "It is not the worst formula on this list by any stretch." / "Before anyone in the comments comes for us, the PC Natural line is a different product and a legitimately good one." CX pb: "That outbreak is more than 15 years behind us. The facility was addressed, ConAgra paid what it owed, and there has not been a comparable incident since. That context matters and is worth stating clearly." CX jam: "To be fair, Craft avoids high fructose corn syrup." CC pb: "The Canadian preparation claim is accurate. The peanuts are dry roasted here. What the marketing does not emphasize is what gets added after the roasting."
g. The named closer ("the reality gap" in CE, an aphorism in the others). CE jam: "The reality gap is this. No Name sold Canadians on the idea that the honest choice was the plain yellow label, but the label tells you almost nothing about where the fruit came from, who processed it, or what percentage of the jar is corn derived sweetener versus actual strawberry. The honest brand is not as honest as it looks." / "The reality gap is the word quality appearing everywhere in the brand identity and nowhere in the formulation." / "When you buy E.D. Smith, the nostalgia is real. The independent Canadian producer behind it is not. That is the reality gap, and it is a wide one." / "The claim is true. The impression it is designed to create is not." CC jam: "The horse-drawn wagon origin story has been marketing for a long time. The ingredient list is the actual product." / "The grandmother implied by the name has never existed. The jam is real. The artisanal story it tells about itself is not." / "The No Name brand exists to be cheap. At that goal, it succeeds. At anything else, it does not try." CE pb: "The devotion is understandable. The ingredient list is harder to defend." CX pb: "The boy who never grows up, the formula that never cleaned up." / "The price is lower. The formula is not." CX jam: "When sugar outranks the fruit on the label, calling it strawberry jam is doing a lot of generous rounding." / "The checkered lid and the jingle and the cursive script are a costume."
h. A callback that stitches beats together. CE jam: "The strawberry jam is even called PC Pure Strawberry Jam. Sound familiar?" CE jam on the two house brands: "Shoppers at Sobeys paying more than they would at No Frills deserve to know they are buying the same sweetener story with a teal logo on top."
i. The bridge to the next number (one sentence, escalating). See 1b.

### 1d. Where the subscribe/like ask sits (measured by cumulative words)
- CC jam: one subscribe ask at 50% (after brand 5 of 7), wrapped in the independence statement: "Before the final brand on the avoid list, a note about what this investigation is and why it matters. The jam aisle sells stories as much as it sells fruit… And the research behind this channel is sourced, fact-checked, and independent of any brand relationship or advertiser revenue. If this investigation is useful to you, subscribe now and turn on notifications. Every video on this channel follows the same standard." (112 w)
- CC pb: 56% (after 6 of 8): "If you are finding value in this investigation, subscribe right now and turn on notifications. Every investigation this channel publishes arrives with sourced facts, no brand revenue, and no advertiser relationships. Subscribe before the final two brands on the avoid list." (41 w)
- CE jam: 57%, immediately before #1, fused with the tease: "We are down to number one, the brand with pure in the product name and glucose-fructose second on the label. Subscribe if you want to keep seeing this kind of breakdown every week, and let us finish this list." (39 w), plus a second subscribe in the sign-off.
- CE pb: 32%, LIKE not subscribe: "We are at the halfway point of the worst list. If this is useful, hit the like button. It takes 2 seconds and it genuinely helps us keep making this content." (31 w); subscribe saved for the sign-off.
- CX pb: 35%, before #2: "If this investigation has been useful so far, tapping the like button helps more people find it, and we are just about halfway through." (44 w incl. the recap line).
- CX jam: two interruptions, 16% (affiliate guide: "Anyone can tell you what to avoid. What most people really need is the replacement… You'll find it in the description.") and 26% (like). This is the lowest-performing video in the set; two early breaks is the anti-pattern.
- → Rule: exactly one mid-list ask, between 35% and 57%, one or two sentences, phrased as helping another shopper or as "let us finish"; subscribe belongs in the sign-off. The 149K video used the shortest ask in the set.

### 1e. The turn to the worth-it picks
- CE jam: "Now for the two brands that actually earn the trust you are already extending to everything else on this shelf." CE pb: "That is the worst list. Now here are two brands doing it right and both of them are genuinely easy to find in Canada." CX pb: "Now that you know what to put back, here are the three that actually held up." CX jam: "Now, let's talk about what actually holds up. And the good news is real because every one of these options is available at stores you already shop at at prices that aren't far off what you're paying now." CC jam: "Two brands earned the worth it placement." CC pb: "Two brands earn the worth-it placement. The first is owned by the same company that makes the most recalled peanut butter on this avoid list, and the formulation could not be more different."
- Picks carry an availability line and a price. CE jam: "Bonne Maman is a French import available at virtually every major Canadian grocery chain. Loblaws, Sobeys, Metro, Costco, and most independent grocers, typically in the five to seven-dollar range for a 370-gram jar." / "You will find Crofter's at Walmart Canada, Costco Canada, and well.ca, typically in the eight to ten-dollar range for a 500-ml jar."
- Picks carry an ownership disclosure when there is one. CC pb: "The ownership disclosure belongs up front because this channel applies the same transparency standard to recommended brands as to avoided ones." CX pb: "One piece of context worth stating clearly, because it will come up, Smucker's is the parent company of Jif. That is a fact, it is public information, and it belongs in this conversation." CX jam on its #1 pick: "Here's the disclosure. This jar deserves. Boniman is not a low sugar product."
- The pick's closing line ties back to the avoid list in ingredient-order terms. CE jam: "Fruit, sugar, pectin, in that order, at that price, at every major Canadian grocery store." CX jam: "When you eat bonaman, you're eating fruit preserved with sugar. When you eat Smuckers, you're eating sweetener flavored with fruit."

### 1f. The "what to look for" close
- CE jam, numbered: "Before you head back to that jam aisle, here is what to actually look for. One, flip the jar. Canadian ingredient panels are ordered by weight, highest to lowest. If glucose-fructose, glucose, or sugar appears before fruit, the jar has more sweetener in it than produce. That is legal. It is just worth knowing. Two, watch the front label language. Pure, natural, traditional, and homestyle have no legal definition on a Canadian jam package… Three, no sugar added is not the same as low sugar… Four, understand what the law requires. Under Canada's Food and Drugs Act, anything calling itself jam must contain at least 45% fruit and hit a Brix reading of 66. Anything below those thresholds must be labeled a spread."
- CX jam, single habit: "Here's the one habit worth changing next time you're in that aisle. Forget the front of the jar. The illustration is not an ingredient. The brand name is not a nutrition fact. The cursive doesn't tell you what's inside. Turn it over. Find the ingredient list. Read the first three items."
- CX pb: "Next time you are standing in that aisle, flip the jar over before you put it in the cart. Read the ingredient list from the top down."
- CC pb: "The most important thing to look for is the list after the peanut."
- CC jam (no list, one line): "Reading the ingredient list is the only honest way to navigate this shelf. Fruit first, then everything that comes after it."
- Run-back: CX pb opens its close with a one-line-per-brand recap ("Seven brands, a 5-year documented contamination history at the same facility…"); CX jam does the same ("Welches and regular Smuckers are running on brand trust… Great Value doubles up on corn syrup… Sam's Choice charges a premium… Craft leads with sugar ahead of the strawberry… Smuckers Sugar-free trades one processed sweetener for a synthetic one"). CC jam's close is a half run-back ("The grandmother on the Bonne Maman jar has never made a jar of Bonne Maman. The horse-drawn wagon that Smucker's built its mythology on has nothing to do with the glucose fructose in the jar."). CE has no run-back.

### 1g. Comment ask
- CE jam: "Drop a comment below with the jam brand you are checking next time you're at the grocery store." CE pb: "If your go-to brand made the worst list, or if there is a Canadian peanut butter we missed, drop it in the comments." CC pb: "Leave a comment with the peanut butter brand in your pantry and we will tell you exactly what is inside it." CX pb/CX jam use a share ask instead: "this is worth sharing with them." / "share it with someone who still has the smuckers in their pantry." CC jam has NO comment ask and still drew 125 comments; the audience volunteered brands (Dora ×5, Last Mountain, Kirkland, Podravka, S&F, PC Blue Menu) — the CC pb wording ("we will tell you exactly what is inside it") is the one that turns that into a follow-up video.

### 1h. Sign-off and disclaimer
- CE jam: "And if you want more of these ingredient breakdowns on everyday Canadian staples, subscribe. We do this every single week."
- CE pb (the only full disclaimer in the set, and it is on the 149K video): "Everything in this video is based on publicly available ingredient data from Canadian retailers, not personal opinions, and not sponsored content… Sources are listed in the description. Individual dietary needs vary. Always read labels and make decisions based on your own health requirements."
- CC jam mantra: "Fruit first, then everything that comes after it." CX pb: "The jar that feels the most familiar is not always the jar that contains what you think it does." CX jam: "The jar made of actual fruit is sitting right next to it on the same shelf for the same price. It's been there the whole time. Now you know exactly what you're looking for."

## 2. WHAT THE 80K CC VIDEO DID THAT THE 27.6K CE VIDEO DID NOT, AND VICE VERSA

### CC did (and CE did not)
1. Brand-history depth with dated ownership chains and deal values, ~100 words per beat: "The family-owned company was sold in 2001 to Imperial Capital. In 2007, it became a wholly-owned subsidiary of TreeHouse Foods, an Illinois-based American company. In October 2022, TreeHouse Foods sold E.D. Smith, along with Knox Gelatin and its pasta business, to Invest Industrial, a European private equity firm, in a transaction valued at approximately $950 million." CE's equivalent is one sentence and stale ("E.D. Smith is now owned by TreeHouse Foods").
2. A three-teaser cold open that promises stories, not just labels (the wagon, the 125 countries, the First World War).
3. A contrarian pick that manufactures the comment section: Bonne Maman and Greaves on the avoid list, with the disagreement pre-empted in the script ("The disagreement is understandable. The avoid placement stands."). CE put Bonne Maman in the worth-it slot and its top two comments simply agreed with it.
4. The independence statement inside the subscribe ask ("sourced, fact-checked, and independent of any brand relationship or advertiser revenue").
5. Ownership disclosure inside the worth-it beat (Crofter's: "Chicago-based private equity firm Frontenac acquired a majority stake in February 2021. In January 2026, Power Sustainable Leos… acquired Crofter's with the Lotka family remaining as long-term partners").
6. A thematic closing recap that repeats the three teasers as payoffs.
7. Length: 3,271 words vs 2,417, i.e. 35% more runtime at higher retention-per-beat (each CC beat is a self-contained story).
8. The "Sold in Canada" title, which drew zero nationality corrections. CE's "Canadian Jam Brands" drew "Smuckers is inherently an American company… You said 'Canadian brands' in your title" (11 likes) and three "Bonne Maman is NOT CANADIAN" comments.

### CE did (and CC did not)
1. The law hook in sentence two: "Canada's own food law requires 45% fruit in anything legally called jam". CC never states the composition standard. The CE audience picked this up and extended it (the 13-year industry insider: "A pure Jam spec is a product with 45% fruit and 65% brix, a pectin Jam is 27% fruit and 65% brix. anything called fruit spread or preserve have no standard to adhere to.").
2. The "pure" device as the spine: "one of them literally put the word pure in the product name while listing glucose-fructose as the second ingredient", paid off at #4 (Compliments Pure), #3 ("PC Pure Strawberry Jam. Sound familiar?") and #1 (Smucker's Pure).
3. A numbered countdown with worst-last and an explicit escalation logic (price/trust rising as the number falls).
4. The named beat closer, "The reality gap is…", used five times so the viewer knows when a beat is ending.
5. Verbatim ingredient declarations with the source named ("from compliments.ca reads, …"; "The confirmed ingredient list for Smucker's Pure Strawberry Jam sold in Canada").
6. Prices with jar sizes for avoid brands and picks ("$3 for a 500 ml jar", "five to seven-dollar range for a 370-gram jar", "eight to ten-dollar range for a 500-ml jar"). CC has no prices at all; the 80K audience filled the gap ("$7.99", "$41 in BC").
7. A numbered "what to look for" list (four items) and a comment ask; CC has neither.
8. Consistent product across the list (strawberry jam, with one slip to raspberry at PC).
9. The subscribe ask fused to the #1 tease ("let us finish this list") rather than a stand-alone note.

### What both got wrong in the same way
Both narrated "sugar/glucose first" over footage showing strawberries first (CC: E.D. Smith and at least one more; CE: Compliments and Great Value). See section 3. Both used "healthiest"/nutrition framing that the house rules now bar (section 4).

---

## 3. EVERY FACTUAL ERROR OR CONTRADICTION VIEWERS CAUGHT, AND THE STRUCTURAL FIX

### 3a. Caught on the CC 80K video
1. E.D. Smith label misread — TOP COMMENT, 48 likes: "at 4.50 the statement (re ED Smith jam) is that 'sugar is the first ingredient and fruit next' …which is not what that 'ingredient list' is showing… please check this." Script line: "E.D. Smith's standard strawberry jam lists sugar first among its ingredients, followed by strawberries, also water, fruit pectin, and citric acid. Sugar is the primary ingredient, which means there is more sugar by weight than fruit." FIX: every first-ingredient claim is read aloud FROM the filmed declaration, on the jar bought for the video, and the frame stays on screen while the words are spoken; a second pass checks narration against footage line by line before export; the "which means more sugar by weight than fruit" inference is only spoken when the filmed order supports it.
2. Same error on more than one brand: "For more than one entry, the visual lists fruit first, but the narration claims sugar first"; "4:48, 7:39 listing shows strawberries first and sugar second". 7:39 falls in the No Name / Compliments stretch, where the script has no declaration at all, only template claims: "Sugar leads the ingredient list, followed by fruit with pectin, citric acid, and water filling in the rest." (No Name) and "Sugar leads the ingredient list. Fruit content is secondary." (Compliments). FIX: no beat without a filmed declaration; if the jar was not bought and filmed, the brand is not in the video. No "same industrial jam template" generalisations.
3. "Please stop with the 'DEADLIEST' captions… try-hard click-bait." (39 likes). Not a script error but a credibility hit on the same video. FIX: thumbnail/caption text comes from a checkable claim in the script ("SUGAR FIRST", "27% FRUIT", "PURE?"), never a mortality or danger word.
4. Smucker's beat read the US formulation: "Smucker's strawberry jam contains strawberries, high fructose corn syrup, corn syrup, sugar, fruit pectin, and citric acid. Count the sweeteners." then contradicted itself: "High fructose corn syrup, which appears on Canadian labels as glucose fructose". A 20-like comment added "Smucker's No Sugar Added uses sucralose", i.e. the audience knows the Canadian jars. CE's Canadian reading is "Strawberries, glucose-fructose, sugar, citric acid, pectin." FIX: Canadian jar only, common name as printed on the Canadian label ("glucose-fructose"), with the FDR prescribed-name point cited on screen. Never a US list on a "Sold in Canada" title.
5. Omissions the audience corrected: Dora (5 commenters: "Made & owned in Canada", "only seen it at Walmart", "meilleure confiture qualité-prix… compagnie Canadienne"), Kirkland Signature organic strawberry (17 likes), St. Dalfour at Costco (19 likes, "$41 in BC" multi-pack), Last Mountain Berry Farms (SK, "available at all Costco and most retailers"), PC Blue Menu Twice the Fruit ("raspberries, sugar (sugar, concentrated blackcurrant juice extract), water…"), E.D. Smith Triple Fruit ("not as sweet… full of fruit flavours"), Podravka and S&F (Croatia) at Food Basics, Dollarama strawberry jam ("strawberries, sugar, pectin, citric acid… made in Egypt"). FIX: worth-it picks and the wildcard line drawn from this list; "Canadian-owned" for Dora/Last Mountain verified from the corporate registry before it is said; Costco availability stated for St. Dalfour/Crofter's/Kirkland.
6. No prices in the video; audience supplied them ("No Name jam is $7.99 in Canada! Use to be $4.00"). FIX: price per 100 g at a named banner on a named date for all nine jars.
7. "Note the low sugar versions are 'spreads,' not jams." (15 likes). The video never used the common name. FIX: the jam/spread interlude (section 5).
8. Loyalty pushback: 79-year-old "buys E.D. Smith at Giant Tiger on sale and will keep doing so" (43 likes); Grimsby resident on Triple Fruit (10 likes). FIX: the concession beat for E.D. Smith names the Winona plant and the Triple Fruit line as printed, so the loyal buyer hears their jar acknowledged before the avoid case.

### 3b. Caught on the CE 27.6K video
1. Compliments misread: "for Compliments you said that glucose came before fruits but it's not true, the label shown put strawberry the first ingredient". Script line: "The official Compliments Pure Strawberry Jam ingredient declaration from compliments.ca reads, "Sugars, glucose, sugar, strawberries, citric acid, fruit pectin."" The website and the physical jar disagreed and the narration followed the website over the footage. FIX: the physical jar is the source of record; if the retailer site differs, say so on screen ("compliments.ca lists X; the jar we bought on [date] lists Y") or leave the site out.
2. Great Value misread: "The Great Value example you show leads with strawberries, then sugars". Script line: "The standard Great Value strawberry jam leads with glucose-fructose before strawberries in the ingredient declaration." FIX: as above.
3. Crofter's location: "Crofters is not based in Guelph, On." Script line: "Based in Guelph, Ontario, Crofter's produces certified organic, fair-trade fruit spreads". CC says Parry Sound. FIX: location read off the dealer line printed on the jar and the corporate registry, not memory.
4. Title nationality: "Smuckers is inherently an American company… You said 'Canadian brands' in your title." and "Bonne Maman is NOT CANADIAN. It is made in France" (×3). FIX: "Sold in Canada" in the title; origin statement as printed ("Product of France", "Prepared in Canada") spoken in every beat.
5. The composition standard: insider (13 years in the industry): "A pure Jam spec is a product with 45% fruit and 65% brix, a pectin Jam is 27% fruit and 65% brix. anything called fruit spread or preserve have no standard to adhere to." Script line: "Under Canada's Food and Drugs Act, anything calling itself jam must contain at least 45% fruit and hit a Brix reading of 66. Anything below those thresholds must be labeled a spread." Three problems: (i) the standard sits in the Food and Drug Regulations (Division 11), not "the Food and Drugs Act"; (ii) the script omitted the second standardised name, "(naming the fruit) jam with pectin", with its lower fruit minimum (the insider's 27%); (iii) the insider's soluble-solids figure (65) and the script's (66) disagree. FIX: read FDR B.11.201 and B.11.202 directly, put the section numbers on screen, use the regulation's own numbers, and say plainly that "fruit spread" carries no compositional standard. The regulation, not the comment and not the previous video, is the tie-breaker.
6. "Pure" may not be an empty adjective in this category. Script line: "The word pure on a food label has no legal definition under Canadian food law." The insider calls the 45% standard "a pure Jam spec", and CC's own history line says E.D. Smith "began producing what is documented as the first domestically made pure jam in Canada". FIX: before the "pure" interlude is written, check the FDR text and CFIA's Industry Labelling Tool for whether "pure" is defined, customary or prohibited on jam; script says only what those two sources say.
7. Cumulative credibility: "A lot of wrong information. Do not trust this video! Check the label instead." (7 likes). FIX: an on-screen source line for every checkable claim (label frame, FDR section, filing/press release, retailer receipt).

### 3c. Contradictions between the two jam videos (and inside them) that the new script must resolve from primary sources
- E.D. Smith owner: CC "In October 2022, TreeHouse Foods sold E.D. Smith… to Invest Industrial" vs CE "E.D. Smith is now owned by TreeHouse Foods". CC's is the later-dated transaction; confirm from the TreeHouse/Investindustrial press release or filing and name the current operating company on screen.
- Crofter's: CC Parry Sound + "Frontenac acquired a majority stake in February 2021" + "Power Sustainable Leos… acquired Crofter's" vs CE "the only brand on this entire list made by a Canadian company that has not been acquired by a US food conglomerate" + Guelph. If a Chicago PE majority stake existed, CE's "not been acquired" sentence is wrong as written. Resolve from filings/press releases; disclose ownership inside the pick, CC-pb style.
- Bonne Maman: CC avoid ("an industrial jam produced by one of Europe's largest fruit processing conglomerates sold at a premium price point") vs CE worth-it #1 vs CX best #1. CC's list: "fruit, cane sugar, concentrated lemon juice, and fruit pectin". CE's list: "strawberries, sugar, cane sugar, concentrated lemon juice, fruit pectin. Five ingredients." One of these is not the Canadian jar. Film it.
- Smucker's Canadian list: CC (US list with HFCS + corn syrup + sugar) vs CE ("Strawberries, glucose-fructose, sugar, citric acid, pectin"). Film it.
- Kraft: CC concedes "The formulation objectively is cleaner than Smucker's" then avoids it on corporate grounds ("It makes jam the way a portfolio management team allocates manufacturing capacity"). An ex-Kraft commenter and another ("KRAFT PURE jam was the best but hard to find now") show the audience does not accept a corporate-context avoid. If Kraft is on the list, the case is label/origin/price, or it is not on the list.
- Internal to CE: the PC beat switches product — "The President's Choice Pure Raspberry Jam carries the ingredient declaration, "Sugars, sugar, glucose, raspberries, pectin, citric acid."" in a strawberry list. One flavour, all nine jars.
- Internal to CE: "In Shatlands blind taste test of Canadian grocery store strawberry jams" — outlet name garbled/unverifiable. Name the outlet and link it, or drop it.
- Internal to CC: "In Canada, the Smucker's brand is limited to ice cream toppings and preserves." Unsourced and at odds with the Smucker's Pure jam line CE quotes. Drop.

## 4. LINES THAT BREAK THE CURRENT HOUSE RULES, AND THE SUBSTITUTE DEVICE

House rules applied: no health, nutrition, calorie, sugar, sweetener-safety or additive commentary; no "healthiest"; no unverified brand histories; allegations labelled. Substitute devices: composition standard (FDR B.11.201/B.11.202), legal common name as printed, ingredient order as printed, origin statement as printed, price per 100 g, ownership from filings. Note the line: reading "sugar is printed first, strawberries second" off a filmed label is a fact and is allowed; saying what that sugar does, how much there is, or whether it is good or bad for anyone is commentary and is not.

### 4a. CC jam (80K)
Health/nutrition/sugar/sweetener/additive commentary:
- "The reality behind most of those jars is industrial production, aggressive sweetening" → "On five of the seven jars, the first word on the ingredient list is sugar or glucose-fructose."
- "And the healthiest recommendation on this list uses a sweetening method…" → "the second worth-it pick prints its fruit content on the jar".
- "Count the sweeteners. High fructose corn syrup, corn syrup, and sugar. Three separate sweetening agents in a single jar" → allowed only as an ingredient-order count read off the Canadian label; the US list goes.
- "A quiet admission that the decision to include it in standard products is a formulation choice, not a manufacturing necessity." → drop; opinion about formulation.
- "A fruit jam requires fruit, sugar, pectin, and something acidic." → replace with the composition standard: "The regulation lets a jar be called jam with X% fruit."
- "The fruit content is lower relative to sugar than any of the worth it options" → fruit percentage as printed where printed; otherwise "not printed".
- "a conventionally formulated sugar-forward jam" → "a jam whose printed list begins with sugar".
- "Bonne Maman does not use high fructose corn syrup, no additives, and no artificial preservatives." / "The lemon juice, rather than citric acid, is a meaningful quality distinction from most industrial jam competitors." → list as printed, no quality verdict on any single ingredient.
- Greaves: "which means it requires more sugar than a pectinized jam to achieve the same set because pectin is what allows jam to gel at lower sugar concentrations… the finished product has a higher sugar content per gram of fruit" / "The formulation delivers a very high-sugar spread" / "fruit-forward, lower-sugar alternatives" → price per 100 g against the picks, common name as printed, origin (Niagara-on-the-Lake as printed).
- Crofter's: "The spreads contain 30 calories per serving with 1/3 less sugar than a conventional jam, achieved by sweetening with fair-trade cane sugar and organic apple juice concentrate" / "Gluten-free, vegan, and produced without artificial preservatives" / "controlled sweetness" → "fruit is the first word on the list; the common name printed is 'fruit spread'; certified organic mark as printed; made in Parry Sound, Ontario as printed; $X per 100 g".
- St. Dalfour: "The healthiest recommendation on this list" / "No cane sugars, no corn syrups, no glucose-fructose, no artificial sweeteners have ever been used in the product's history" / "No preservatives, no artificial colors, no artificial flavors" / "the cleanest, lowest-sugar, most fruit-forward jam option" / "earns the healthiest placement" → "the label prints its fruit per 100 g; ingredient list as printed; Product of France as printed; Costco multi-pack price per 100 g".
- Close: "Heritage claims do not alter the sugar content." → "Heritage claims do not alter the ingredient order."
Unverified brand histories (all need a filing, press release or the company's own page, and attribution "the company says"):
- "The J.M. Smucker Company was founded in 1897 by Jerome Monroe Smucker, an Orville, Ohio farmer who built a cider mill and started selling apple butter from the back of a horse-drawn wagon." / "By 1980, J.M. E. Smucker was the number one jams and jellies company in the United States with over 25% of the American market."
- "Ernest D'Israeli Smith was born in 1853 in Winona…" / "in 1904, E.D. Smith began producing what is documented as the first domestically made pure jam in Canada" / "sold in 2001 to Imperial Capital" / "approximately $950 million" / "employing around 240 people".
- "a product line the company launched in 1978" (No Name); "Sobeys introduced the Compliments label in 2005".
- "Bonne Maman was created in 1971 by Jean Gervoson… recovering unsold plums from his father-in-law's fruit holdings" / "available in over 125 countries and is the top-selling preserves brand in France" / "hundreds of millions of jars annually".
- "Greaves Jams and Marmalades was founded in 1927 by Mabel and William Greaves" / "55 Queen Street in a building that dates to approximately 1845".
- "Gerhard and Gabi Lotka grew up in rural Germany, where Gerhard's father owned a food flavor and extraction company" / "the largest producer of organic fruit spreads in North America" / "Frontenac acquired a majority stake in February 2021" / "In January 2026, Power Sustainable Leos…".
- "St. Dalfour is a privately owned family company that has operated under the same ownership since its founding in 1984" / "the grandmother of Mr. Kistner" / "over 80 countries" / "It has never made product for any private label or store brand".
→ Substitute: ownership and dates from filings/press releases only (the 2022 TreeHouse divestiture, the Crofter's transactions if real); founding lore either cut or fenced as "the company's own telling is…".
Allegations/opinion unlabelled:
- "Grocery store ownership of jam production allows the retailer to capture margin from consumers who trust the brand without verifying what is in the jar." → drop or "our reading is".
- Kraft: "It makes jam the way a portfolio management team allocates manufacturing capacity." / "The jam is not the product. The product is the shelf space and the brand equity" → cut; avoid case must be label/origin/price.
- "In Canada, the Smucker's brand is limited to ice cream toppings and preserves." → cut (unsourced).

### 4b. CE jam (27.6K)
- "Glucose-fructose is Canada's functional equivalent to American high-fructose corn syrup, a processed corn derived sweetener that manufacturers use because it is cheaper than cane sugar and improves shelf stability." / "you're buying a sweetener product with fruit flavoring, not the reverse" → "glucose-fructose is the common name the Food and Drug Regulations prescribe for this ingredient; it is printed second."
- "older formula tracked by Open Food Facts also carried sodium benzoate and added flavor, signals of industrial formulation" → drop (crowd-sourced source, additive commentary).
- "It swaps refined sugar for sucralose and introduces sodium benzoate, a preservative the regular line does not use. The healthy-looking option is actually the more additive-heavy one." → "the No Sugar Added jar's printed common name is 'spread', not 'jam'; that is the whole difference the label is required to show you."
- "It is a high-intensity corn derived sweetener. It is inexpensive, industrially processed, and shelf-stable." / "The processing method and the metabolic profile are functionally equivalent" → cut entirely (sweetener-safety/metabolic).
- "roughly 30% less sugar than conventional jam" / "brings the added sugar per tablespoon down to approximately 4 g compared to 10 to 12 g" → cut; fruit first as printed, three ingredients as printed, price per 100 g.
- "If you are spreading jam on your kids' toast every morning, this is the jar to reach for." → cut (health-adjacent advice); "if you want the shortest printed list at a mainstream price, this is it".
- "Three, no sugar added is not the same as low sugar or simple ingredients… The label looks healthier. The formulation is more complex." → replace with "read the common name".
- Unverified: "Great Value jam is produced by rotating contract manufacturers" / "No Name jam is a Loblaws house brand produced by third-party contract manufacturers" / "approximately 37% of PC items formally identified as prepared in Canada by Loblaws" / "E.D. Smith is now owned by TreeHouse Foods" / "Based in Guelph, Ontario" / "In Shatlands blind taste test" / "Health Canada regulates what must be disclosed, not which adjectives" (labelling enforcement is CFIA; check) → origin statement as printed ("Prepared in Canada", "Product of France"), ownership from filings, outlet named or cut.
- "The Compliments jam carries… quality at everyday fair prices" is fine (quoted marketing); "a labeling strategy, not a quality signal" is opinion and should be flagged as ours.

### 4c. CE peanut butter (149K)
- "hiding hydrogenated oil, corn syrup fillers, and multiple sweeteners" / "the hydrogenated stabilizers" / "Corn maltodextrin is a highly processed carbohydrate derived from corn starch used as a bulking agent and texture modifier. It adds nothing nutritionally and is functionally a filler." / "Hydrogenated vegetable oil is the stabilizer…" / "Mono- and diglycerides are emulsifiers." / "one of the most heavily processed formulas" → ingredient count and order as printed, nothing about function.
- "Canadian shoppers on Reddit grocery threads frequently note…" / "The dominant complaint in Canadian consumer reviews…" / "Canadian shoppers on Reddit's personal finance threads consistently flag it" / "blind taste tests consistently describe it as rich" → drop, or a named outlet with a date.
- "the brand has a documented history of using contract manufacturers whose standards have not always held up to scrutiny" → unlabelled allegation; cut unless a named record.
- Keep as models: the per-100 g line, "voted Canada's most trusted peanut butter brand in the 2025 BrandSpark Canadian Trust Study" (cite the study), the disclaimer minus its last clause ("Individual dietary needs vary. Always read labels and make decisions based on your own health requirements." → "Labels and prices change. Read the jar in your hand.").

### 4d. CX peanut butter (113K, US)
- Health/safety: "Aflatoxin is a naturally occurring carcinogen…" / "Independent testing found Jif to contain 6.28 micrograms per kilogram of aflatoxins" / "The American Heart Association specifically recommends limiting it because of its saturated fat content and its link to elevated LDL cholesterol" / "For a diabetic who reached for the reduced fat jar…" / "A 2 Tbsp serving of reduced fat Jif saves approximately 15 calories" / "Nutritionists who formally assess commercial peanut butter quality consistently place Jif at the bottom" / "Pregnant women who need the aflatoxin concern addressed" / "Anyone managing a cancer diagnosis" → all cut; none has a Canadian substitute beyond origin/ownership/price.
- Records stated as fact without a document on screen: "5-year contamination history that the FDA put in a formal warning letter" / "In 2022, Skippy recalled more than 161,000 lb" / "In 2007, a salmonella outbreak linked to Peter Pan peanut butter sickened more than 700 people across 47 states" → allowed only as "per the FDA warning letter dated…" / "per the CFIA recall notice dated…" with the document shown.
- Company claims as fact: "tests the peanuts before production begins and then tests the finished peanut butter again… Every batch, twice. The results are published." → "the company says it…".
- Unverified histories: "Teddy has been making peanut butter in Massachusetts since 1928" / "Peter Pan has been on the shelf since 1928" / "The one that held the number one position for 30 years".

### 4e. CX jam (21K, US)
- Calories/sugar per serving in every beat: "One tablespoon runs around 50 calories with roughly 10 g of sugar. Almost all of it added." / "roughly 9 g of added sugar. That's nearly a fifth of most adults recommended daily cap" / "approximately 12 g of total sugar, roughly nine of which are added. Most health guidelines place the daily added sugar cap for adults somewhere between 25 and 36 g" / "A tablespoon runs around 8 g of sugar" / "Approximately a third less sugar" → all cut.
- Fibre/nutrition: "Sam's Choice Strawberry Rhubarb Preserves contains zero grams of dietary fiber." → cut; substitute is the common name and fruit percentage if printed.
- Sweetener-safety: "sucralose, the lab synthesized compound sold commercially as Splenda, roughly 600 times sweeter" / "a faint chemical trailing note" / "Sucralose does not spike blood glucose" / "diabetics, people monitoring carbohydrates" → cut; "the jar's common name is 'spread'".
- Health-adjacent claims for picks: "meets USDA organic standards", "no added cane sugar at all", "Every gram of sweetness in this jar is sitting next to real fruit" → certification mark as printed; ingredient order as printed.
- Taste/texture verdicts ("Wobbly where it should be firm… translucent in a way that crushed grape simply isn't") → not a house device; cut.
- Affiliate interruption: "We spent months comparing hundreds of products and put the better options… into one simple guide. You'll find it in the description." → never.
- Unverified histories: "Welches has been pressing Concord grape since 1869" / "Walmart's house brand launched in 1993" / "Smuckers has been on American kitchen tables since 1897" / "It started in France in 1971".

### 4f. CC peanut butter (24.5K)
- "the healthiest recommendation is a small family operation in Ontario" / "The healthiest recommendation on this list has been making one-ingredient peanut butter" → "the second worth-it pick".
- "Health Canada banned partially hydrogenated oils in 2018 because of their trans fat content" / "still adds saturated fat and represents an industrial modification of the product's natural fat profile" / "not for nutritional honesty" / "Peanut butter's primary nutritional value lies in its fat, specifically the mono-unsaturated and poly-unsaturated fats that make peanuts genuinely beneficial for cardiovascular health" / "approximately 45 calories per serving with around 90% less fat" / "It is not a nutritionally neutral substitute" / "healthy fat, protein, fiber, and micronutrients" / "the nutritional profile that made peanuts worth eating" → all cut.
- "the environmental concerns attached to palm oil sourcing are not resolved" / "the effectiveness of that certification system is subject to ongoing scrutiny" → unlabelled; cut or "critics say, per [named report]".
- Unverified histories throughout: "In 1946, a Kentucky businessman named William T. Young created a peanut butter product he called Big Top" / "Skippy was first sold in 1932 by an Alameda, California food packer named Joseph L. Rosefield" / "Adam's peanut butter has been around since 1922" / "Nuts to You Nut Butter was founded in 1989 in Paris, Ontario, a small town where the Grand and Nith Rivers meet" / "Fatso Peanut Butter was founded in 2016 by Jill Van Gin" / "Peanut Butter & Co. was founded in 1998 by Lee Zalben" → filings/press releases or cut.
- Recall stated well (a model for allegations labelled): "In May of that year, J.M. Smucker recalled Jif products manufactured at the Lexington plant due to potential salmonella contamination. The recall affected Canada and the United States." → keep the form; add "per the CFIA recall notice" on screen.

### 4g. The one sentence to copy as the house voice
CC jam's own concession pattern is already compliant: "The avoid placement is not about authenticity or craftsmanship." Rewrite the rest in that register: "The avoid placement is about what the label prints, where the jar says it was made, who the filings say owns the name, and what it costs per 100 grams."
