## 5. RECOMMENDED SKELETON (21,000–23,000 characters ≈ 3,500–3,750 words at the CC ratio of 6.2 chars/word)

TITLE: 7 Jam Brands Sold in Canada You MUST AVOID (And 2 That Are Actually Worth It)
Thumbnail text drawn from a script fact ("SUGAR FIRST" / "27% FRUIT" / "PURE?"); no "DEADLIEST".
One flavour for all nine jars (strawberry), one banner per price, one date, receipts kept. Every declaration filmed.

ORDER: countdown 7→1 from least to most popular, most popular last. "Popular" must be a stated, checkable proxy, said once on screen (e.g., number of national banners stocking it plus the retailer's own best-seller rank on the shoot date, or a named trust study if one covers jam). Candidate roster after research (least → most): 7 Great Value (Walmart only) · 6 Compliments (Sobeys banners only) · 5 No Name · 4 President's Choice · 3 Kraft or Bonne Maman (decide on proxy; Bonne Maman is the audience's favourite, so if it is in, its case is origin/ownership/price only and the concession is generous) · 2 E.D. Smith · 1 Smucker's. Worth-it candidates: Crofter's (fruit first as printed, made in Parry Sound as printed, organic mark, Costco/Walmart price per 100 g; disclose ownership from filings), plus Dora (Canadian-owned per registry, Walmart) or St. Dalfour (fruit per 100 g printed, Product of France, Costco). Wildcard line: Kirkland organic strawberry / PC Blue Menu Twice the Fruit / Last Mountain Berry Farms. Roster locked only after every jar is in hand.

BEATS (word budgets; characters ≈ words × 6.2):
1. COLD OPEN (~190 w). The shelf lie in one line (CC: "one of the most misleading stretches of shelf space in the entire building" register) → the law number (CE: "Canada's own food law requires 45% fruit in anything legally called jam", corrected to the regulation's own figures and section) → "and there is a second standard that lets a jar be called jam with far less fruit; you have probably never seen the words" → count: "Seven brands on the avoid list today, two that earned genuine worth it placements" → hook: "Number one is the jar with the word pure on the front of it. It is last. Don't skip ahead."
2. YARDSTICK (~220 w, ends "write that number on your hand" per the olive note). Four checks the viewer will use seven times: the common name printed under the brand (jam / jam with pectin / fruit spread / preserves), the first three words of the ingredient list, the origin line (Prepared in Canada / Product of France / Made in Canada from domestic and imported ingredients), price per 100 g. State the ingredient-order rule once, as law: CE's "Canadian ingredient panels are ordered by weight, highest to lowest."
3. #7 → #5 (3 × ~270 w). Beat anatomy, fixed order: (a) recognition/shelf hook (b) owner + banners (c) price per 100 g (d) common name as printed (e) "flip the jar": first three ingredients read off the filmed frame (f) origin line as printed (g) ownership from filings (h) the concession (i) the closer, CE-style "The reality gap is…" or one CC aphorism (j) one-line bridge up the ladder.
4. INTERLUDE, "the word that fools you" (~250 w), placed between #5 and #4 so it precedes the first "Pure" jar. Content: jam vs jam with pectin vs fruit spread vs preserves, with the FDR section on screen and the fruit minimums from the regulation, "fruit spread carries no compositional standard" (the insider's point, verified), the CC commenter's observation that "the low sugar versions are 'spreads,' not jams" made in our words; then "pure": only what the FDR/CFIA text supports (either "a customary trade name for the higher-fruit standard" or "not defined"), never CE's unverified "no legal definition" line unless the text says so. Ends with the callback device to reuse three times: "Sound familiar?"
5. #4 → #3 (2 × ~270 w). Same anatomy. The "Pure" callback lands on PC and/or Compliments.
6. LIKE ASK (~40 w) immediately before #2, phrased as a favour to another shopper, CE-pb length: "If this is useful, hit the like button. It takes 2 seconds" register; no subscribe here.
7. #2 (~290 w). E.D. Smith if the roster holds: Winona plant and Triple Fruit acknowledged as printed (the 79-year-old's jar), ownership chain from the 2022 divestiture filing/press release, current label read off the frame — this is the beat that was misread at 4:50 last time, so the frame holds for the full sentence.
8. #1 (~340 w). Most popular. Full anatomy plus the "pure" payoff: CE's structure ("It is right there in the product name on every jar, and it is doing an extraordinary amount of work") delivered with the Canadian list only ("Strawberries, glucose-fructose, sugar, citric acid, pectin" if that is what the filmed jar prints), the FDR prescribed common name point, and the closer: "The claim is true. The impression it is designed to create is not." register.
9. THE TURN (~50 w). CE: "Now for the two brands that actually earn the trust you are already extending to everything else on this shelf."
10. WORTH-IT #1 and #2 (2 × ~260 w). Each justified by checkable facts only: common name printed, fruit first (or fruit per 100 g printed), origin line, ownership from filings disclosed up front (CC-pb: "The ownership disclosure belongs up front because this channel applies the same transparency standard to recommended brands as to avoided ones."), banners, price per 100 g against the avoid median. Closing line in CE's form: "Fruit, sugar, pectin, in that order, at that price, at every major Canadian grocery store." One wildcard sentence after the second pick for the audience's other brands.
11. COUNTER TEST (~140 w), addressed to the viewer, never "we tested": read the common name under the brand; read the first ingredient; find the fruit percentage if printed (EU-made jars usually print "prepared with X g of fruit per 100 g"; Canadian-made jars usually do not, and that absence is itself the finding); divide the shelf price by the grams.
12. RUN-BACK (~120 w). One line per brand, 7→1, in label terms only (CX-pb form: "Seven brands, …").
13. THREE QUESTIONS AT THE TILL (~60 w). "What does the label call it? What is the first word on the list? Where does it say it was made? If the jar can't answer all three, put it back."
14. COMMENT ASK (~40 w). CC-pb form: "Leave a comment with the jam brand in your pantry and we will tell you exactly what is inside it." Invite the missing Canadian brands by name (Dora, Last Mountain) so the follow-up video writes itself.
15. MANTRA (~15 w). "Read the label, not the ad." or CC's own "Fruit first, then everything that comes after it." Pick one, repeat it once in the yardstick and once here.
16. DISCLAIMER (~40 w). CE-pb form, rewritten: "Everything in this video is read from labels bought at Canadian retailers on [date], the Food and Drug Regulations, and public filings. Not sponsored. Sources in the description. Labels and prices change; read the jar in your hand."

Running total ≈ 190+220+810+250+540+40+290+340+50+520+140+120+60+40+15+40 = 3,665 words ≈ 22,700 characters. Trim avoid beats to 255 w if the first draft lands over 23,000.

RESEARCH GATES before drafting: (1) nine jars bought, receipts, declarations filmed; (2) FDR B.11.201/B.11.202 text read and the fruit/soluble-solids figures copied from it; (3) CFIA guidance on "pure"/"jam with pectin"/"fruit spread" read; (4) ownership for E.D. Smith, Crofter's, Smucker's Canada, Bonne Maman (Andros), Greaves, Dora, Last Mountain from filings/press releases/corporate registry; (5) popularity proxy chosen and documented; (6) FDR prescribed common name for glucose-fructose confirmed.

## Canada Eats, "6 Canadian Jam Brands to Never Buy And 2 That Are Great" — 27,566 views, 401 likes, 54 comments, 3 May 2026
- Top comments (20, 18 likes): Bonne Maman is the one they buy / the best.
- 12 likes: Crofter's "read the sugar percentage. No additives like sucralose either."
- 11 likes: "Smuckers is inherently an American company… You said 'Canadian brands' in your title."
- 7 likes: "A lot of wrong information. Do not trust this video! Check the label instead."
- Errors called out: "for Compliments you said that glucose came before fruits but it's not true, the label shown put strawberry the first ingredient"; "The Great Value example you show leads with strawberries, then sugars… Compliments also lists strawberries first"; "Crofters is not based in Guelph, On."
- Insider (13 years in the jam industry): "A pure Jam spec is a product with 45% fruit and 65% brix, a pectin Jam is 27% fruit and 65% brix. anything called fruit spread or preserve have no standard to adhere to."
- Bonne Maman "is NOT CANADIAN. It is made in France" (three comments).
- Dora "Canadian made and owned… only seen it at Walmart"; Last Mountain from Southey SK; Crofter's at Save-On-Foods.

## What this tells the script
1. The single most-liked comment on the 80K video is a label misread. Every "first ingredient" line in the new script must be read off the retailer's own ingredient declaration and filmed.
2. The audience's own picks: Bonne Maman, Crofter's, St. Dalfour, Dora, Kirkland organic, PC Blue Menu Twice the Fruit, Last Mountain, E.D. Smith Triple Fruit. Dora and Last Mountain are Canadian-owned brands the channel has never covered.
3. The audience knows the "jam vs spread" distinction and the 45% rule; the insider gave the 27% "jam with pectin" number — the script should be the one that gets the standard exactly right.
4. "Canadian brands" in a title draws corrections when the brand is American; the new title says "Sold in Canada".
5. Thumbnail text "DEADLIEST" drew a 39-like complaint.
